A run the log holds no line of answers `run_missing: true` as well - in a RAM journal a run from before the last boot is gone, and the pages say so for a run that has finished. **The pages** (task 178): a read is the newest `limit` entries (500 without a limit; the Log page asks for 1000), and the answer says whether the log goes on past the page: **`older`** - there are entries older than the first line answered - and **`newer`** - entries newer than the last one at the moment of the read (the reader is asked for one entry more than the page and it is cut). **`before=<cursor>`** is the `limit` entries older than a line's `cursor` (journald: `journalctl -r --after-cursor=`, turned oldest first again; the ring buffer: the line's place in it, or its stamp when the buffer has moved past it), **`after=<cursor>`** the `limit` entries newer than it, **`head=1`** the oldest `limit` entries of the boot or the range (`journalctl -n +N`); one of the three at most, or `422 invalid`, as is a cursor that is not one (a journal cursor `s=…;i=…;b=…;m=…;t=…;x=…`, or a dmesg line's `<monotonic_us>/<k>`, `k` its ordinal among the lines with that stamp). A cursor the journal cannot seek to is journalctl's error in `error`; one the ring buffer does not know is an empty page. So the whole boot is read in pages: the tail, then `before=` the first line's cursor until `older` is false; or `head=1`, then `after=` the last line's cursor until `newer` is false. A read that fails answers 200 with no lines, `error` and `source`. when the journal's copies are on a share (or a stick) that is mounted and the system's own user cannot read them (a root-squashed NFS export), a journald read (not following) answers **`copies_unreadable: {path, error, hint}`** as well: the lines are the RAM journal alone, and the Log page says so. |
| `GET /log/stream[?…]` **`logs:read`** | journald only: the same filters as `/log` (`kernel`, `boot` and `run` included, `422 invalid` for a bad boot or run; an earlier boot has nothing new to follow), streamed as Server-Sent Events (`data: <line JSON>`), `limit` lines of history first (`0` = none) and then every new entry until the client closes. **`after=<cursor>`** (task 178) replays the entries since that cursor first (`journalctl --after-cursor=`), so a stream opened after a page joins it without a gap; `before` and `head` are `422 invalid` here. Busybox answers 501; the UI polls `/log` there. |
| `GET /log/download[?tag=&unit=&severity=&since=&until=&q=&kernel=&boot=&run=&format=text\|json]` **`logs:read`** | A GET like `/log`: the log as a file — the shell fetches it with a one-time ticket (`?ticket=`), never with the session in the URL. `/log`'s filters without its `limit` (a `limit` is ignored), always oldest first; with no range everything the journal holds (journald) or the whole `/var/log/messages` (busybox, where `unit`, `since` and `until` are ignored as on `/log`; its rotated generations are not part of it). `format=text` (the default) answers `text/plain; charset=utf-8`, one line per entry in `journalctl -o short-iso`'s shape: `2026-09-12T11:05:30+02:00 <host> <tag or unit>[<pid>]: <message>` — the offset with a colon, as systemd 258 prints it; the tag is the syslog identifier journalctl shows, the unit only where there is none; the further lines of a multi-line message indented under its start, so a line at the left edge is always a new entry. A busybox line keeps its own time (`Sep  6 03:46:30`, no year) and leaves out `facility.severity`; a line syslogd did not format is its text alone. `format=json` answers `application/x-ndjson`, `/log`'s line objects one per line. `Content-Disposition: attachment; filename="<host>-log-<from>-<to>.<txt\|jsonl>"` with times as `2026-09-12T1130` in the system's zone: a range open at its end runs to the download time, one open at its start is `until-<to>`, no range (always on busybox) is the download time alone; an expression the name cannot resolve stands in it reduced to letters, digits, dots and dashes. `kernel=1` downloads the kernel log — on busybox the ring buffer — and names the file `<host>-kernel-…`, `kernel=0` names it `<host>-system-…` (journald); a `boot` of the journal adds `-boot-<the id's first eight digits>` (an offset as `minus1`) after `log` or `kernel`. The entries go from `journalctl -o json` (or the file) to the response one at a time — the journal is never held in memory — and the headers go out before the first entry. **The file stops at 50 MB** and then ends with a cut line — text `-- Cut off here: the download stops at 50 MB. … --`, JSON `{"cut":"limit","limit_bytes":52428800,"message":"…"}`; a read that fails midway (journalctl exiting non-zero, an unparsable `since`) ends the same way with `"cut":"error"` and the reason in the message, so a cut file never passes for a complete one. `422 invalid` for another `format`, a bad `boot` or a bad `run`, `501 unsupported` where the log reader cannot export. Authentication as on every route: either session cookie, the `Authorization` header, or `?sid=` — which a plain browser link from a session without a cookie (the system's local token set as the UI's session) needs. |
| `GET /boots` **`logs:read`** | Any session: the boots the journal holds, newest first — `{current, source: "journald"\|"syslog", persistent, boots: [{index?, boot_id, first?, last?, current, journal, snapshot?, total_ms?}], error?}`. Read from `journalctl --list-boots -o json` (systemd 258 answers `[{index, boot_id, first_entry, last_entry}]`, the stamps in microseconds; the text table of older versions is read as well, its times in the system's zone) and kept for 30 s. `current` is `/proc/sys/kernel/random/boot_id` without its dashes, the form journalctl prints. `index` is journalctl's offset, 0 the newest boot. An earlier boot's `first` and `last` are its first and last entry in the journal, **except that a `first` older than the boot's start is that start**: the last entry's `__REALTIME_TIMESTAMP` less its `__MONOTONIC_TIMESTAMP` (such a system writes an earlier boot's first entries with the image's date too), looked up once per boot while occulited runs, at most 8 new lookups a request; **the running boot's `first` is now − `/proc/uptime`**, because a system without a clock writes its first entries with a wrong time (the Pi 4's RAM journal began "on 13 March", measured 2026-09-12). `persistent` says whether the journal is on the userfs now (the bind mount over `/var/log/journal`, as `GET /journal` reads it): in RAM the journal holds this boot only, and the Log page's boot menu says so. A journal that has not written this boot yet gets it first in the list; when journalctl fails there is this boot alone, and `error`. Busybox: this boot alone, `source: syslog`, `persistent: false`. **Kept boots** (the boot snapshots below): a boot whose timeline is kept carries `snapshot: true` and its startup's `total_ms`; a kept boot the journal no longer holds follows the journal's boots with `journal: false` and without `index`, its `first` the snapshot's `started`. `journal` is true for the journal's boots and for this boot. The Log page offers the boots with `journal`, the Services page's timeline the kept ones. Measured 2026-09-12: the x86_64 VM's persistent journal lists 28 boots, the Charly's and the Pi 4's one each; a boot's kernel log has 654 lines on the VM, 526 on the Charly and 551 on the Pi 4, as many as `dmesg -r` holds a quarter of an hour after the boot. |
| `GET /boot[?id=]` **`logs:read`** | Any session: the startup timeline of this boot, the Services page's boot order graph (below the timers). `{boot_id, started?, finished, timestamps: {kernel, initrd?, userspace, finish?, multi_user?}, summary: {kernel_ms, initrd_ms?, userspace_ms?, total_ms?, multi_user_ms?, slowest?: {id, duration_ms}}, units: [{id, description?, activating, active?, inactive?, duration_ms, state, sub?, after?, restarted?}], critical_chain: [], later, later_units?}` — times in seconds after the kernel started. **Read with two `systemctl show` calls**, the manager's `InitRDTimestampMonotonic`, `UserspaceTimestampMonotonic` and `FinishTimestampMonotonic`, and `systemctl show --all -p Id,Description,LoadState,ActiveState,SubState,InactiveExitTimestampMonotonic,ActiveEnterTimestampMonotonic,ActiveExitTimestampMonotonic,InactiveEnterTimestampMonotonic,After '*'` for every loaded unit (without `--all` a oneshot that has run and exited is not listed), because `systemd-analyze` is not on every image (the x86_64 OVA has none). **Computed as `systemd-analyze` computes it:** a unit is in the timeline when it began to start (`InactiveExitTimestampMonotonic` set) no later than the manager finished; a later start — a timer's oneshot, a restart — counts in `later`. `duration_ms` is active − activating, else inactive − activating (a oneshot that ended, a unit that failed), for a unit still starting the time so far. `summary` is `systemd-analyze time` (the kernel until the initrd or userspace, the initrd, userspace until the finish, the total), multi-user.target's `ActiveEnterTimestampMonotonic` and the slowest unit. `critical_chain` is `systemd-analyze critical-chain multi-user.target` with its fuzz of 0, in the tool's order: from the root, among the units it is ordered after that became active by the finish, the ones that became active last, and on from each of them — a unit reached again is named, not followed twice. `after` lists the units of the timeline a unit is ordered after; `systemctl show` quotes a name holding a backslash (`"systemd-fsck@dev-disk-by\\x2dlabel-userfs.service"`), which is unquoted. `started` is now − `/proc/uptime`. **Checked against `systemd-analyze`** on the captures of 2026-09-12: on the Charly and the Pi 4 the chain, its `@` and `+` times, `systemd-analyze time` and the top of `blame` agree to the millisecond. **Cost:** the call over every unit took 0.7 s on the x86_64 VM, 1.8 s on the Pi 4 and 3.5 s on the Charly, about 90 KB (2.4 s without the device units, which the chain ends on): the timeline is kept once the boot has finished, and for 5 s while it is still starting; the page reads it only when its section is opened. **This boot as it happened, its units as they are:** once this boot's snapshot is kept (below) this boot is answered from it (without `snapshot`), not from a fresh read - systemd keeps only a unit's last start, so after occulited itself restarted a fresh read had lost every unit restarted since the boot, occulited included. Before that, the first read of the running process. Either way each unit's `state` and `sub` are live, from `systemctl show --all -p Id,ActiveState,SubState,InactiveExitTimestampMonotonic '*'` (kept 30 s; about 4 s on the Charly), and a unit whose last start began after the boot finished carries **`restarted`**, when it did (seconds after the kernel started). **`later_units`** names the units counted in `later`: loaded units whose last start began after the boot finished and that are not in the timeline - on a timeline read after occulited restarted without a snapshot, the lost ones. An `id` naming an earlier boot answers its kept timeline, with `snapshot: true` (`404 no-timeline` when it was not kept); an offset or anything else is `422 invalid`. **`previous`** is `{boot_id, started?, recorded_ms, total_ms?, userspace_ms?}`, the boot kept before the one answered — for this boot the newest kept boot other than it — which the page compares with (absent when there is none); `501 unsupported` off systemd; `500` with systemctl's error when a call fails. |
| *(the boot snapshots)* | Unit timestamps exist for the running boot only, so occulited keeps each boot's timeline: once the boot has finished starting (`FinishTimestamp` set) and the wall clock can be trusted — the boot timing's check, `/run/occulite/clock-state` saying `rtc` or `ntp`, or chronyd's `Leap status : Normal`, since a kept boot is named by when it began — it writes `<state-dir>/boots/<boot id>.json.gz`: `GET /boot`'s answer plus `recorded_ms`, gzipped (the Charly's 209 units are 51 KB of JSON, 7.5 KB gzipped), through a temporary file and a rename, once per boot. occulited looks every 15 s after its start and gives up after 30 minutes (a unit that never stops starting, a clock never synchronised) with a log line. **The last 10** by `recorded_ms` stay, on every product, the SD card ones included; older ones are removed after each save. The directory holds `.nobackup`, so the nightly backup (`tar --exclude-tag=.nobackup`) leaves it out. A file that cannot be read is left out of the lists and logged. **The no-network record** (openccu-lite B-249, occulited B-34): for the first minutes after boot occulited samples `eth0`'s `carrier` and `operstate` and the network start's `/var/status/hasLink` and `hasIP` every 5 s, and at 3 minutes of uptime it writes `<state-dir>/boots/<boot id>.net.json` (`0640`) when the network is not working then - `reasons`: `no-carrier` (eth0 never had one), `carrier-lost` (it had one and has none now), `no-eth0`, `no-lan9514` (a Raspberry Pi 2/3 Model B by the device tree's model, and neither eth0 nor the chip - hub `0424:9514`/`0424:2514`, Ethernet `0424:ec00`/`0424:7800` - in the USB tree: the warm-reboot hang), `no-link-status`/`no-ip` (the status LED's no-network: a status file missing, where `/var/status` exists). A carrier on a `wlan*` interface keeps a record from being written unless the chip is missing. `{interface, reasons, board?, written, uptime_s, final, carrier_at_s?, samples: [{uptime_s, carrier, operstate, has_link, has_ip}], links: [{name, operstate?, carrier?, flags?, driver?, device?}], usb: [{path, id, product?, speed?, authorized?, drivers?}], usb_entries: [names], lan9514: {hub?, ethernet?, driver?}, controller?: {device, hprt0?, busconnected?, buspower?}, lan_run?: {gpio, direction?, value?}, lan_reset?: {key: value}, addresses?: [{name, addrs}], routes?: [{iface, destination, gateway?, metric}], counters?: {rx_packets, tx_packets, rx_errors, tx_dropped, …}, network_log?: [lines], kernel?: [lines]}` - `usb` is the USB tree from sysfs with each device's interface drivers and `usb_entries` the plain `ls /sys/bus/usb/devices`, `lan9514` where the chip's hub and Ethernet are in it, `controller` dwc_otg's port files as they read, `lan_run` the chip's reset line (the GPIO named `LAN_RUN` in the device tree; direction and value only while it is exported - it is never exported for this), `lan_reset` the fork's boot-time reset's `/run/lite-lan-reset`, `addresses` and `routes` (IPv4 and IPv6, from `/proc/net`) what `ip addr` and `ip route` show, `counters` eth0's statistics, `network_log` this boot's journal lines of `occu-lan-reset.service` and `occu-network.service` (udhcpc's among them, the last 200), `kernel` this boot's kernel lines about USB, `smsc95xx`, `eth0`, dwc_otg and KFENCE (the last 200). The samples go on after a first carrier; the record is written again, `final: true`, at 6 minutes, with `carrier_at_s` when a first carrier came after the first record. Nothing is done about the network here. It is kept whether or not the boot's timeline is (such a boot may never get a trusted clock), goes with its boot when that is pruned, and the newest ten of boots without a timeline stay. `GET /boot` carries it as `network`, for this boot and a kept one; a start of occulited later than 3 minutes after boot watches nothing. |
| `GET /timers` **`system:read`** | systemd only: `{systemd: true, timers: [{unit, activates, next, last, left, active, own, enabled, unit_file_state}]}` from `systemctl list-timers --all` plus one `systemctl show -p UnitFileState` for all of them — the recurring jobs (backup, firmware and addon update checks, catalogue stars). `own: true` marks the timers made on the Services page (`local-<name>.timer`, below); a stored own timer systemd has not loaded (switched off and dropped from memory since) is listed too, after the others, with `active: false`. `enabled` and `unit_file_state` as on `GET /services` (a runtime-enabled own timer says `enabled-runtime`, a switched-off one `disabled`). Busybox answers `{systemd: false, timers: []}`. |
| `GET /timers/own` **`system:read`** | **Own timers**, systemd only (busybox 501 `not-systemd`): `{timers: [{name, unit, service, timer_file, service_file, enabled}], analyze}`. `name` is the short name, `unit` is `local-<name>.timer`, `service` `local-<name>.service`, the two `_file`s are the stored texts, `enabled` is false when the timer was switched off on the page. `analyze` says whether `systemd-analyze` is on the system — the images do not ship it today (`BR2_PACKAGE_SYSTEMD_ANALYZE` is off): without it the files are not verified before they are applied (systemd's own load check below still is), and the calendar check answers `available: false`. |
| `POST /timers/own` **`system:write`** | `{name, timer, service}` creates an own timer; 201 with the timer as in the list. `name`: 1 to 32 of `A-Z a-z 0-9 - _`, starting with a letter or a digit (`local-<name>.timer` is accepted as well). The units are always `local-<name>.timer` and `local-<name>.service` — the prefix keeps them apart from every shipped unit, and it is all the privilege helper admits in `/run/systemd/system/` besides the override drop-ins. What is saved is the text of both files, so any directive can be written. **Refused, and nothing written**: a bad name (422 `invalid`); a stored own timer of that name, or any unit systemd can already load under either name (409 `exists`); a file that is empty, over 64 KiB, carries a NUL or lacks its section, `[Timer]` or `[Service]` (422); what `systemd-analyze verify` rejects when it is installed (422, its output as `message`). Then the texts are stored as `<state-dir>/units/local-<name>.timer` and `.service` on the userfs, written to `/run/systemd/system/` through the helper, `daemon-reload`ed and checked with `systemctl show -p LoadState,LoadError`: a unit systemd did not load (a broken `OnCalendar=`, a timer with nothing to trigger on) is 422 with the load error, and **taken back out** — both files removed from `/run` and the userfs, reloaded again. Last `systemctl --runtime enable --now local-<name>.timer`. The stored timers are written, reloaded and enabled again at every start of occulited, before the enable/disable switch is replayed. |
| `GET /timers/own/{name}`, `PUT /timers/own/{name}`, `DELETE /timers/own/{name}` **`system:read` / `system:write` / `system:write`** | `{name}` is the short name or `local-<name>.timer`; 404 `not-found` for one that is not stored. `PUT {timer, service}` replaces both files with the same checks; a text systemd does not load puts the previous one back in both places (422), and an enabled timer is restarted so that a changed schedule is armed at once. `DELETE`: `systemctl --runtime disable --now` on the timer, `stop` on its service, both files removed from `/run` and the userfs, `daemon-reload`; `{ok: true}`. Switching an own timer off and on is `POST /services/local-<name>.timer/disable` and `…/enable`. |
| `POST /timers/own/{name}/run` **`system:write`** | Starts `local-<name>.service` once, now, without waiting (`systemctl start --no-block`): `{ok: true, output}`. The outcome is in the log under that unit. |
| `POST /timers/calendar` **`system:write`** | `{expression}` → `{available, expression, normalized?, next?, output?}` from `systemd-analyze calendar --iterations=3 -- <expression>`: `normalized` is systemd's normalized form, `next` the next three run times as systemd prints them (`Sat 2026-09-12 03:00:00 CEST`), `output` its whole answer. `available: false` (200, nothing else) when `systemd-analyze` is not installed. 422 `invalid` with systemd's complaint as `message` for an expression it cannot parse; an empty expression, one over 256 characters, with a line break, or starting with `-` is refused without running anything. A POST, so administrators only. |
| `GET /nav` **`system:read`** | Menu entries for the shell: `nav.d` drop-ins, plus one per addon that brings a **web frontend of its own** — a lighttpd drop-in in `/usr/local/etc/config/lighttpd/<id>.conf` mapping a path to the addon's server, and that path is the entry's `href`. An addon's `Config-Url` is its *settings page*, a different thing, and is **not** a menu entry: it is reached from the Addons page. **`id` and `addon`**: `id` is the entry's route, `/nav/<id>`; an addon's synthesised frontend is named after the first path segment its drop-in proxies — RedMatic's Node-RED at `/addons/red/` is `red` — lower-cased and reduced to `[a-z0-9-]`, 32 characters at most. `addon` is the addon the entry belongs to (`redmatic`), absent for a `nav.d` entry that is no addon; the shell keys the dropdown row, the settings link (`/addon-settings/<addon>`), the favicon and the kept page by it, and replaces `/nav/<addon>` by `/nav/<id>` when the two differ, the query string kept. The addon id stays the `id` where the path's name is ambiguous: two frontends on one path, a `nav.d` entry of that name, or another installed addon's id. A `nav.d` drop-in keeps its own `id` (with `addon` set when it is named after an installed addon). Standalone is the `href` itself; a bare `/addons/<addon>` goes to occulited's addon files (see [Addon CGIs](#addon-cgis)), which answer 404 for it, as for a directory without an index. `source` says what an entry is rather than where it was read: `addon` (an addon's frontend, and the shell's addon dropdown) or `nav.d` (a configured menu entry that is no addon, listed in the shell's addon dropdown under the addons — the tab bar is Status, the pinned addons, Addons and System only). A `nav.d` drop-in named after an installed addon is a deliberate declaration of that addon's frontend and is reported as `addon`, keeping its own `href`, label and target. A `nav.d` drop-in may set `"keep_alive": true` to have the shell keep its page loaded while other pages show — the entry then carries `keep_alive: true`, never one that opens in a new tab (`target`) — as it does for an addon's frontend (see *Kept pages* in the embedding contract below). **`session_header: true`** marks an addon's entry whose installed version reads the gate's `X-Occulite-Session`: its stored manifest says `ui.session_header` (the manifest is per version, so the flag is a boolean; for an addon without a stored manifest the `runtime.session.header_since` the old catalogue stored in its policy still counts against the installed version). The shell opens its `href` without `?sid=@…@`, in the frame and in a new tab. Nothing is fetched for this. It is absent for an addon without either declaration, an unknown installed version and an `href` outside `/addons/`; the shell then appends nothing on its own. **`legacy_session: true`** marks an entry whose `href` the shell opens with the session's legacy alias as `?sid=@…@`: an addon's page that occulited serves, not proxied to the addon's own server (a CGI named by the addon's own `nav.d` drop-in) without `session_header` while the legacy session is switched on for the addon (`GET /legacy-session`: globally, and not in its `off` list), and a `nav.d` page under `/addons/` while the global switch is on. **Never a frontend proxied to the addon's own server** (the path its lighttpd drop-in proxies, synthesised or named by its drop-in): such a server learns the session from the gate's `X-Occulite-Session` and can do nothing with the alias — the API refuses it, so a frontend that checks its `?sid=` against the API refuses the page, which is what Homematic Manager did on the Charly. Absent, the URL stays plain. See [addons.md](https://github.com/hobbyquaker/openccu-lite/blob/main/docs/addons.md#settings-page-or-frontend). |

### occulited's version

occulited's version is **the commit it was built from** (occulited task 16), the full hash, in one of these forms:

| Form | Example | Built from |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `<hash>` | `fa42dfde1e31fb074df53220dd573ceb92642ff0` | the commit: every image (its pin), and `scripts/build.sh` on a clean tree |
| `<hash>-dirty` | `fa42dfde1e31fb074df53220dd573ceb92642ff0-dirty` | `scripts/build.sh` on a tree with uncommitted changes |
| `<hash>-hot` | `fa42dfde1e31fb074df53220dd573ceb92642ff0-hot` | a binary deployed onto a system by hand (`VERSION=` given to the build) |
| `dev` | `dev` | a bare `go build`, a build outside git |

The bare hash is beside it (`occulited_commit` on `/status`, `commit` on `/health` and on the meta API's `/version`),
absent for a build that carries none. The version names a commit, not a release, and is not ordered: what a system
runs as a whole is the image's version, `release` on `/health` and the `LITE=` line of `/VERSION`. Every image build
round still tags the occulited commit it pins with the image version (`v1.0.0-dev.40`, annotated), so the tag says which
occulited went into which image; the binary does not report it. `1.0.0-dev.38` to `1.0.0-dev.40` reported that image
version (or `git describe`'s `1.0.0-dev.38-5-gfa42dfd` between rounds) as the version instead (occulited task 9, taken
back by task 16).

### Storage health: the verdict

`GET /storage` judges each disk and answers the worst of them, with the reasons of that
level. The thresholds are named constants in occulited's `internal/system/storage.go`; the panel
draws its wear bars with the same 80 % and 100 %.

| Verdict | `code` | When | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `replace` | `smart-failed` | SMART's overall health is *failed* | the drive's own verdict |
| `replace` | `emmc-eol-urgent` | eMMC `pre_eol_info` `0x03` (urgent) | the reserved blocks are nearly used up |
| `replace` | `emmc-life-exceeded` | eMMC `life_time` A or B `0x0B` | past the rated life |
| `replace` | `wear-exceeded` | SMART wear 100 % or more | the SSD twin of eMMC's `0x0B` |
| `replace` | `io-errors` | one storage error in the kernel log of this boot (`count`) | a disk that fails reads or writes now is the one that loses data next |
| `watch` | `emmc-eol-warning` | `pre_eol_info` `0x02` (warning) | 80 % of the reserved blocks consumed |
| `watch` | `emmc-life` | `life_time` A or B `0x09` or `0x0A` (`percent` the range's lower bound, 80 or 90) | the same 80 % as the SSD rule |
| `watch` | `wear` | SMART wear from 80 % (`percent`) | NVMe *percentage used* or a SATA SSD's used life; the last fifth of the rated endurance |
| `watch` | `reallocated`, `pending`, `media-errors` | one reallocated or pending sector, one NVMe media error (`count`) | a drive that has started to lose blocks |
| `watch` | `ext4-errors` | a filesystem's `errors_count` of one or more (`count`; `filesystem` its first mount point) | the counter survives fsck: the errors happened, whatever repaired them |
| `watch` | `sd-age-writes` | an SD card at least 5 years old by its CID date that receives at least 500 MB a day, over at least 3 days of figures (`years`, `bytes_per_day`) | SD cards report no wear, so age and load are all there is. A system in normal use writes 4 to 17 MB a day ([base-scope.md](https://github.com/hobbyquaker/openccu-lite/blob/main/docs/base-scope.md)); 500 MB is something else writing — a persistent debug journal, a database addon — and on an old card that combination is worth watching. Figures from the first hours after a boot are mostly the boot, hence the 3 days |
| `good` | | none of the above | |

Not used: the SCSI `ioerr_cnt` (it read `0xd` on the OVA's healthy disk — it counts the
passthrough commands a probe sends), and SD vendor health through CMD56 (`mmc-utils` is not in the
image, and every vendor has its own format).

### Status page warnings

A warning is an **id plus a variant**: a change that matters is a new warning, and a silence of the
old one does not hide it. The ids, in the order the page lists them within a severity:

| id | variant | severity | active when | `params` | `href` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `rega` | the addon ids, sorted, comma-separated | error | an installed addon needs the ReGa (`rega_dependent` in `/addons`), disabled or switched on again | `addons: [{id, name, enabled}]` | `/addons` |
| `arch` | the same | error | an addon's binaries cannot run on this system (`binary_incompatible`, and not `rega`) | the same | `/catalog` |
| `addon-ended` | the same | warning | An installed addon that keeps a daemon and whose unit is empty (`ended` in `/addons`); the variant is the ids | the same | `/addons` |
| `crash-loop` | the units, sorted, comma-separated (`rfd`, `addon-mosquitto`) | error | openccu-lite task 283: a unit restarted 3 times within 10 minutes (a supervised addon: 15) and has not stayed active for 120 s since. The core units (occulited, occulited-helper, lighttpd, rfd, hmipserver, multimacd, hs485d, hmlangw, sshd, chrony, ssdpd) are sampled every 15 s from systemd's `NRestarts`; systemd's backoff (2 s doubling to 5 min, no start limit) never lets such a unit reach the failed state. The addons whose runtime declares `daemon: true` are **supervised**: when their unit is active with an empty cgroup (`ended`), occulited restarts it (`systemctl restart addon-<id>`: the rc.d `stop`, then `start`) after 2 s doubling to 300 s, and those restarts count. **Not while an addon install or uninstall runs** (occulited B-30): the job stops, starts and settles the units itself, so the supervisor restarts nothing meanwhile, and after the job an empty unit starts its backoff afresh. The LED's `service-failed` state names the same units | `units: [{unit, restarts, total, since, result, addon}]` | `/system/services` |
| `occulited-crash-loop` | the loop's first failure, Unix seconds | warning | task 283: occulited itself was in a crash loop - its unit's state file (`/run/occulite/occulited-state.json`, written by systemd's hooks, `deploy/systemd/occulited-unit-state`) says 3 or more failures in a row, the last within 24 hours | `fails, restarts, result, first, last` (RFC 3339) | `/system/log?unit=occulited` |
| `addon-payload` | the same | error | An installed addon's program files are gone (`payload_missing` in `/addons`, not dismissed) — after a restore, since a backup carries no `.nobackup` directory; its unit is skipped, not failed (B-267); such an addon is not also `rega` or `arch` | the same | `/addons#reinstall` |
| `meta` | occulited's start, Unix seconds | error | the metadata store was recovered from its backup at this start | — | `/metadata` |
| `unclean` | the marker's time, Unix seconds | error | `/var/status/uncleanShutdown` is there | `at` (RFC 3339): the marker's time, or this boot's start (now − `/proc/uptime`) when the marker is older than that - it was written before the clock was set; the variant stays the marker's time | `/log?boot=-1` when the journal lists the boot before this one (the log routes resolve the offset to its id); `/log?since=@<at − 3600>` where it does not (RAM, no journal, journalctl failing) |
| `backup-target` | the path | error | the nightly backup is on and its directory cannot be read (only while a directory target is set) | `path` | `/backup` |
| `backup-userfs` | the path | error | the nightly backup is on and writes onto the userfs (`on_userfs`) | `path` | `/backup` |
| `backup-unencrypted` | `nightly` | warning | Backups leave the system unencrypted (no recovery key, or the switch off) while the nightly run is on with an enabled target, or a network target exists — anyone who reads the stick or share reads every key of the system | — | `/backup#encryption` |
| `backup-delivery` | `<target id>:<cause>` | error; `too-old` warning | An enabled target whose last test or delivery failed — `cause` is the state (`unreachable`, `auth-failed`, `host-key-changed`, `host-key-unknown`, `read-only`, `full`, `stale`, `no-sftp`, `no-medium`, `update-room`, `last-failed`) — or, while the nightly run is on, no successful backup to it for 26 hours (`too-old`, the backstop for every cause) | `name`, `cause`, `detail` | `/backup#targets` |
| `journal-target` | the mode (`ram-sync`, `persistent`), or `usb` | warning | The setting (or the product's default) asks for ram-sync or persistent, `/var/log/journal` is not mounted, and the boot script left its reason in `/run/occu-journal/fallback` — only once it has tried, so the minutes before it runs at boot are no warning. Variant `usb` while ram-sync's USB stick is not plugged in (`label`, `dir`; `path` is the target) | `mode`, `reason`, `path`, `label`?, `dir`? | `/log?settings=journal` |
| `store-target` | `usb`, `failed` | warning | The database's location is a USB stick and the stick is not plugged in (`usb`), or the last copy to it failed (`failed`); the history stays on the userfs | `label`, `folder`, `reason` | `/log?settings=history` |
| `journal-sync` | `failed` | warning | Ram-sync is chosen and in effect, and its last copy of this boot failed (`sync.state`); a copy that works again ends it, and before the first copy of a boot it is unknown. With a stick target `target: "usb"` and its `label` | `reason`, `at`, `target`?, `label`? | `/log?settings=journal` |
| `certificate` | `renewal-failed`, `expired`, `expiring` | error | in that order: the last ACME renewal failed; the live certificate has expired; fewer than 14 days are left (or `GET /certificate` says `expiring`) | `days` | `/certificate` |
| `storage` | `watch`, `replace` | warning for `watch`, error for `replace` | The verdict; a verdict below `replace` without the kernel log (`kernel_log: false`) is unknown | `verdict`, `reasons` (as in `/storage`), `devices: [{name, kind, model}]` | `#storage` |
| `addon-ownership` | the addon ids, sorted, comma-separated | warning | a confined addon has a root-owned entry in its directories (below); not an addon a restore left without its program (`addon-payload` names it: its unit, and the ownership step in it, did not start - B-267) | `addons: [{id, path}]` | `/services` |
| `legacy-session` | the addon ids, sorted, comma-separated | warning | An installed addon receives the session's legacy alias in a URL the shell opens — its settings page or a page of its own that occulited serves, never a frontend proxied to the addon's own server (`legacy_session` on `GET /addons` and `GET /nav`); gone when none does: every such addon declares the header, or the switches are off. A switch re-evaluates at once | `addons: [{id, name, enabled}]` | `/addons` |
| `console-reset` | the resets, `<user>@<unix time>`, sorted, comma-separated | warning | `occulited admin reset-auth` reset accounts' access on the console in the last 7 days (occulited task 14: passkeys removed, a one-time password set; `console_reset` in `users.json`); gone after the week | `accounts: [{user, at}]` | `/system/users` |
| `security-key` | `default` | warning | the radio detection (`occu-init-rf-hardware`) has finished in this boot, BidCos-RF is there (a module in `/var/hm_mode`, or rfd answering it), rfd answers (the radio sampler's last poll, taken after the detection, listed a BidCos-RF interface), and `crypttool -v -t 0` says the default key is in use — not on an HmIP-only system (no module, and a poll after the detection without BidCos-RF); unknown while the detection runs, and while rfd is down or not asked since the detection | — | `/radio#security-key` |
| `hmip-key-declined` | the SGTIN and the time of the newest decline, `<sgtin>@<RFC 3339>` | warning | Hmipserver turned an inclusion request down because the device's local key it was given (in `sgtin.map`, or entered for the pairing) is not that device's key — its line in this boot's journal, read with journalctl's own grep on the fixed part of it. One warning per device. It goes when that device is paired after all (the line stays in the journal, so nothing else can clear it); unknown while hmipserver does not answer `listDevices` | `sgtin`, `address`, `at` | `/system/keys#device-keys` |
| `hmip-adapter` | the marker's `code` (`adapter-exchange-rejected`) | error | Hmipserver's last start failed on a known fatal error — eQ-3's key server rejected the adapter exchange — and the marker `/run/occulite/radio/hmipserver.fatal` is there, so the unit waits for the next run of the radio stack (a re-detection, a hotplug that changes the plan, a connection change; *The adapter exchange* below). Gone with the marker; a different code is a new warning | `adapter` (the SGTIN hmipserver was started on), `line` (hmipserver's line), `cause` (`unreachable`, `refused`; empty for an older marker) | `/system/interfaces#connections` |
| `hmip-local-swap` | the module and the move's time, `<SGTIN>@<RFC 3339>` | error | The newest entry of the local record of adapter exchanges (*The adapter exchange* below) is a move onto the module HmIP-RF runs on now that failed with `cause: adapter-version` (openccu-lite B-289): the module's application firmware is below 2.8.0, hmipserver moved the access point onto it without the network key and called it a success - HmIP-RF runs, but the module cannot send, and eQ-3's key server refuses every exchange away from it. Gone once HmIP-RF runs on another module (the way back from a kept identity) or a later entry is the newest | `module`, `from` (the previous module), `version` (the module's application firmware when the record has it), `minimum` (`2.8.0`) | `/system/interfaces#connections` |
| `hmip-module-missing` | the module's SGTIN | error | On *Automatic*, HmIP-RF is kept on the module that holds the HmIP network (openccu-lite task 318, D-120: the module it last ran on, recorded per board in `/usr/local/var/lib/occulite/hmip-module.json`), and that module is missing or did not answer at the last run of the radio stack: hmipserver runs its VirtualDevices half alone, and no other module is taken unasked - another would make hmipserver attempt the adapter exchange and rewrite the identity files. `params.module`; the link goes to the Interfaces page's connections, where moving HmIP-RF to another module is the confirmed connection change. Gone once the module is back or HmIP-RF is moved by choice. |
| `devices-import` | the HmIP move's state (`pending`, `unknown`, `no-module`) | warning | A device import brought the HmIP identity of another module (openccu-lite task 275), and hmipserver has not taken it over onto this system's module yet - `GET /radio/import`'s outcome; `params.from`, `params.module`, `params.count`. A rejected exchange is `hmip-adapter`'s; the warning goes when the move is done or the record is dismissed. |
| `radio-load` | `dc`, `cs` or `dc,cs` | warning | The radio's load is high (openccu-lite task 316): the duty cycle of an interface above 50 %, or its carrier sense above 10 %, in the radio sampler's last poll; cleared when the value has fallen 5 points below the threshold (the hysteresis). Separate from the LED's radio levels, whatever those are set to. `params: {duty_cycle, carrier_sense, interface}` - the highest values and the interface of the highest. | `/radio` |
| `hb-rf-eth` | the configured address | warning | An HB-RF-ETH is configured (`/etc/config/hb_rf_eth`) and its kernel module is not connected — the board does not answer, or its module is not loaded; occulited tries it again every 30 s through the radio hotplug. `params.address`; the link goes to the LAN devices page's board section (`/system/lan-devices#hb-rf-eth`; the Interfaces page's until then). |
| `radio-firmware` | `<device>@<newest version>` | warning | (the boot never flashes the coprocessor): a module's newest file on the system is newer than what it runs (verdict `newer-available`). `params: {device, running, newest, drops_off?}` - `drops_off` for an HM-CFG-USB-2 below 0.967. A notice only: nothing flashes on its own; the link goes to `/system/updates#radio-firmware`. |
| `radio-module-unusable` | the node | warning | An HmIP-RFUSB the radio detection found but could not read (verdict `unusable`): the system has no radio on it until it is flashed. `params: {device, node, newest}`; the link goes to the radio firmware section, where the flash is offered. The Interfaces page says it too. |
| `addon-update` | every `<id>@<available version>`, comma-joined | warning | An installed addon has a newer version - from the catalogue's latest releases as the system holds them (`catalog.Service.Cached`: nothing is fetched for the warning; the Addons page's check and its *Check daily*, refresh them) and, for an addon the catalogue does not know, from its own update check (`/addons/updates`). `params: {count, addons: [{id, name, installed, available}]}`, `href: /addons`. It clears when the updates are installed or a check no longer finds them; a newer version after a silence is a new variant. The Addons tab and its *Manage addons* entry show it as a **yellow** dot; **red** is `addon-failed` or `addon-ended`; no other warning lights that dot. |
| `catalog-check` | `failing` | warning | The addon catalogue's check keeps failing (occulited B-52): three checks in a row, or failed checks over a day (two daily runs) - the user's check and the daily one count alike; one failed check is the Addons page's alone, which says it in its header and in a notice with the reason and a retry. `params: {failures, since, detail, host}` - the count, the first failure (RFC 3339), the last one's message (`<host>: <cause>`) and the host; `href: /addons`. Read from `GET /catalog`'s `check_error` as the system holds it; nothing is fetched for it. It clears with the next check that works; a silence lasts until then. |
| `addon-failed` | the addon ids, comma-joined | error | Enabled addons whose unit is in systemd's failed state. `params: {addons: [{id, name, enabled}]}`, `href: /addons`; the Addons dot is red. |
| `rpc-stalled` | the interface | error | An interface process is held by a callback listener - kind `delivery` always (no event arrives, whether the listener is found yet or not), kind `calls` only once a listener is found (without one, the interface's `down` says enough). `params: {interface, kind, since, checked, listeners}`, `listeners` those that hold it (`no-answer`, `holds`, `blocked`); the kind `listener` - hmipserver's log names a listener that does not take its events, the other clients get theirs - is always a warning; the link goes to the Interfaces page's `#stalls`, where an administrator ends the connection. |
| `firewall` | the families whose rules are not loaded, `ipv4`, `ipv6` or `ipv4,ipv6` | error | A family's INPUT chain has no jump to `lite-input` — the rules were not loaded, or were flushed since, so that family accepts what its policy says whatever the rules. Nothing while there is no rule file yet (occulited converts and loads it at its start) or without iptables; a read that failed is unknown, not clear; the answer is kept for 30 s. The tracker's boot gate keeps a clear reading from spending a silence until the boot has settled | `families` | `/system/firewall` |
| `classic-rpc-open` | what is open: `plain`, `tls` or `plain,tls` | warning | Classic RPC is on with `auth: none` — whoever the firewall lets in controls every device, as on a CCU without authentication. A warning, not an error: it can be meant, and then it is silenced; opening more after a silence is a new warning | — | `/system/remote-access` |
| `app-public` | the account | warning | The Control app's public mode is on (`public.enabled` on `GET /remote-access`, `auth.public` in `occulited.json`) — anyone who reaches the web port operates the house as that account; a changed account is a new warning | `account` | `/settings#public` (the Settings page; `/system/remote-access#public` leads there) |
| `hmip-port-open` | the port | warning | Hmipserver's HTTP port (`hmServerPort` from `/var/etc/HMServer.conf`, 39292 by default) has a listening socket on an address other than the loopback (`/proc/net/tcp`, `tcp6`) — the image's bind shim (`libbindlo.so`) did not load, or the image has none. The firewall still closes the port; the warning says that its second guard is missing | `port`, `addresses` (the addresses, comma-separated) | `/system/firewall` |
| `hmip-local-key` | the check's start, RFC 3339 | error | Local key mode is on and the last switch's check ended `failed` — most watched devices unreachable, or none heard from again (*HmIP local key mode* below) — so an entered key was probably not the network's; a later switch that fails again is a new warning | `unreachable` (the watched devices not heard from again, `total − heard`), `total` | `/system/keys#local-key` |

The `href` is the page where the matter is handled, as the shell's own in-app path. The shell shows the system pages under `/system/<page>` (`/system/backup`, `/system/certificates`,
`/system/services`, `/system/log?…`) and rewrites the paths above to them on the client; the hrefs
occulited sends are unchanged, so a client that follows them lands on the same page as before.

**Silences** are per user and set and lifted by administrators only; each is
`{id, variant, by, at, until}` in `<state_dir>/warnings.json` ([config.md](config.md)). A silence
ends at the **earlier** of two events: `until` passes (1, 7 or 90 days after it was set), or the
warning's condition clears. The second is noticed without anybody reading: occulited evaluates every
source at start, **every five minutes**, and on every `GET /warnings` and silence request, and
deletes a silence whose warning it finds inactive — a warning gone on day 30 and back on day 60
shows again on day 60. For the list ids (`rega`, `arch`, `addon-ownership`) a silence covers a
warning whose addons are all in the silenced list: one addon less stays silenced, one more is a new
warning. A source that cannot tell this time — the addon listing failed, the radio detection has not
finished or rfd is down or not polled since it, crypttool could not be run (its answer is kept for 30 s, and forgotten when the key is set), the
live certificate could not be read, the ownership check has not finished — shows nothing for its ids
and **keeps** their silences: unknown is not cleared. A silence of an id this occulited does not know
is dropped; the silences of a deleted user run out or clear like any other. **While the system is still
starting** — until systemd's manager has its `FinishTimestamp`, or for at most 15 minutes of
uptime — a warning found inactive keeps its silences as well: the backup target's USB stick may not be
mounted yet, the journal's boot script may not have run. Active warnings are shown as usual, and a
silence still ends at `until`. A system without systemd counts as started.

**Root-owned addon files**. For every addon whose policy is `confined`, occulited looks
through `/usr/local/addons/<id>`, `/usr/local/etc/config/addons/<id>`,
`/usr/local/etc/config/addons/www/<id>` and the policy's `data_dirs` for an entry owned by uid 0 —
what a direct `/bin/install_addon` or an addon's own updater wrote as root, which its next start as
`addon-<id>` may fail on. One `lstat` per entry; symlinks are not counted (`chown -R` leaves a link's
own owner, and that owner grants nothing), a directory occulited cannot read is skipped, and a walk
stops after 2,000,000 entries without a result. It runs in the background: a result stands for an
hour unless the addon's rc.d script, its directory or `hm_addons.cfg` changed (an install), or the
entry it named is no longer root's (a `chown` by hand clears the warning at the next read). occulited
itself does not repair at start; `POST /addons/{id}/ownership` is the fix for files changed
while the addon runs. **The unit does, before every start**: the fork's generator gives every
confined addon unit `ExecStartPre=+-/usr/libexec/occu/lite-addon-own <id>`, which runs
`occulited -addon-own <id>` as root over the same directories - by descriptors, following no link,
on one file system, changing only entries with another owner - and writes
`addon-own: <id>: N of M entries given to addon-<id> (<uid>) in S s` to the unit's journal when it
changed something. `<id>.owned` beside the policy keeps a digest of the directories' inodes and change
times after a walk that found everything right; the next start walks again only when it differs.

### Addons and the catalogue

| | |
| --- | --- |
| `GET /addons` **`system:read`** | Installed addons from `/usr/local/etc/config/rc.d/*` `info` and `hm_addons.cfg`: id, name (the `Name:` of `info`, else the entry's `CONFIG_NAME` in `hm_addons.cfg`, else a built-in name for an addon that declares none — `97NeoServer` is *NEO Server* — and the id only when there is nothing else), version, config URL, update URL, operations. **`config_url`** is the addon's `Config-Url` — or, where the addon's stored manifest names `ui.settings_url` (for an addon without a stored manifest the `runtime.settings_url` the old catalogue stored in its policy), that page: Homematic Manager's `Config-Url` is the CCU's button into its app, its settings page `settings.cgi?cmd=config`; `settings.config_url` keeps the raw `Config-Url` of `hm_addons.cfg`. On a systemd system each entry also carries `policy_mode`, `policy_source` and `undeclared`. **`session_header: true`** marks an addon whose settings page (`config_url`, else the one in `hm_addons.cfg`) the shell frames without `?sid=@…@`, by the rule of `GET /nav`. **`fullscreen: true`** marks an addon whose stored manifest declares `ui.fullscreen` (occulited task 24): its frontend offers its own way back, so the Settings page offers to show it as the whole window; the user's choice is the `fullscreen` flag on the addon's entry in `/me/preferences`, and the shell shows the frontend without its top bar only when both say so. **`legacy_session: true`**: the shell frames the settings page with the session's legacy alias as `?sid=@…@` — no `session_header`, and the legacy session switched on for the addon (below). On a systemd system, **`start_early_declared: true`** marks an addon whose declaration (its stored manifest, else its stored block) says `runtime.start: "early"`, and **`start_early: true`** one that starts early at the next boot: declared, and the early start not switched off for it (`GET /early-start`). **`lighttpd_rejected: {reason, line?, statement?}`** (occulited task 23) marks an addon whose lighttpd fragment (`etc/lighttpd.conf` in its tree, B-120) the last sync refused, so nothing of it is in use: the verdict as the sync left it in `/usr/local/etc/config/lighttpd/<id>.conf.rejected` — `reason` the sentence, `line` the first line of the refused statement (1-based) and `statement` that statement shortened to one line, both absent for a verdict on the whole fragment (a link out of the tree, a file too large, a block not closed). It stays until a sync — an install, a start of lighttpd — accepts a fragment of the addon; the Addons page shows it as the red badge *web configuration refused* with the verdict, and the install's output carries the same `[lighttpd]` line. **`payload_missing: true`** marks an addon whose program files are gone — a CCU backup leaves out every `.nobackup` directory, so a restore brings the addon back by its rc.d entry, `hm_addons.cfg` line, settings and data alone, and its unit fails; **`payload_missing_dirs`** names them (relative to `/usr/local/addons/<id>`), **`reinstall_dismissed: true`** that the user dismissed the hint at this version. **`images`** (openccu-lite task 100) names the icon and logo the addon's stored manifest declares, kind → URL on this origin (`{"icon": "/api/system/v1/addons/<id>/images/icon?v=<version>", "icon-dark": …, "logo": …, "logo-dark": …}`, the declared kinds only; absent for an addon without a manifest or without images); `GET /services` carries the same on a generated `addon-<id>` unit. How the system knows: `createBackup.sh` runs tar with `--exclude-tag=.nobackup`, which drops a tagged directory's contents but keeps the directory and the tag — so after a restore every tagged directory holds nothing but `.nobackup`. Any of three signals (openccu-lite B-267): the addon's unit was **skipped** by the fork's program check (`ExecCondition=+/usr/libexec/occu/lite-addon-payload <id>`, run as root, so it sees the directories a confined addon closes to others; the unit stays inactive, never failed — **`skipped: true`** on the entry); the addon-rc wrapper's `<id>.script` link points at a file that is not there; or the addon's tagged **program** directories (depth ≤ 2 under `/usr/local/addons/<id>`; `tmp`, `cache` and what is under `var` are caches, not the program) that occulited can list **all** hold only the tag (one emptied directory, a cleared cache, is not). `payload_missing_dirs` names the emptied ones and those occulited cannot list, else the dangling link's target; it may be empty. Nothing is reinstalled automatically. Nothing is recorded ahead; `<state-dir>/addon-payload.json` holds the dismissals alone and goes when none is left. The Addons page lists them under *Addons to reinstall after the restore*, each with *Installed before the restore; reinstall it.*, with *Reinstall* (the catalogue's `POST /catalog/{id}/install`) where the catalogue knows the addon and has a release for this architecture, *install it by hand* otherwise, *Reinstall all*, and *Dismiss*; the Status page carries the `addon-payload` warning. |
| `GET /addons/stream` **`system:read`** | openccu-lite B-297: Server-Sent Events that tell an open shell when the installed addons or its menu may have changed: one `event: addons` with `{revision}` at once and again on every change, `: ping` every 30 s. The revision is a number that starts from the clock at occulited's start (a restart never repeats one a client has seen) and grows with every change; the client reads `GET /addons` and `GET /nav` again when it differs from the one it saw last. A change is sent at once by the routes that make one - an uninstall (also a failed one), the end of an install job (`POST /addons/install`, `/addons/install/local`) and of a catalogue install (`POST /catalog/{id}/install`, also a failed one), `enable`/`disable` - and, for what changes beside occulited (`install_addon` on the console, an addon's own updater), within about 3 s from a look at the files while a stream is open: the names, modes, sizes and times in `rc.d`, `nav.d` and the addons' lighttpd drop-ins and `hm_addons.cfg` (no rc.d script runs). Since occulited B-53 the shell reads the revision from `GET /stream` (below); this route stays for programs. |
| `GET /stream?topics=addons,service-messages,pairing` **`system:read`** | occulited B-53: **the shell's stream** - the addons' revision, the service messages and the pairing requests in one connection, so a browser holds one long-lived connection for the shell instead of two or three per window (plain HTTP gives a browser six connections per host across all its windows, and lighttpd's HTTP/2 eight streams at once per connection). `topics` comma-separated or repeated, at least one: `addons` sends `event: addons` as `GET /addons/stream` does, `service-messages` `event: messages` as `GET /service-messages/stream`, `pairing` `event: pairing` as `GET /api/auth/v1/pairing/stream` (that topic needs **`auth:admin`** besides; without it `403` naming the scope). Each at once and again on every change, `: ping` every 30 s; an unknown or no topic is `400`. A feed the system lacks (no service-message store, no pairing) sends nothing. The public mode may open it with `topics=service-messages` alone. The shell holds one for the whole browser in a SharedWorker (a window without one holds its own while shown), with the topics its shown pages want, and closes it a few seconds after no window is shown. The single routes stay for programs. |
| `POST /addons/{id}/reinstall-dismiss` **`addons:write`** | Hides the reinstall hint for the addon at its current version — `{ok, id, version}`; the record drops the dismissal once the program files are back or the version changes. 404 for an unknown addon, 409 `not-missing` when its files are there, 501 without the record. |
| `GET /legacy-session`, `PUT /legacy-session` **`system:read` / `auth:admin`** | The legacy session's switches, `occulited.json`'s `addons.legacy_session` and `addons.legacy_session_off` ([config.md](config.md)). `GET` (any session): `{enabled, off: [ids]}` — `enabled` is the global switch (on by default), `off` the addons it is switched off for. `PUT` (**administrator**): `{enabled?, off?}` — a key left out keeps its value; `off` is cleaned to valid ids, each once, sorted. Answers the `GET` view and re-evaluates the warnings at once. `501` when the daemon runs without a configuration file (then every such addon gets the alias). The shell's switch is the Addons page's section *Addon sessions* (`/addons#addon-sessions`). |
| `GET /early-start`, `PUT /early-start` **`system:read` / `addons:write`** | The early addon start's switches, `occulited.json`'s `addons.early_start` and `addons.early_start_off` ([config.md](config.md)). `GET` (any session): `{enabled, off: [ids], next_boot: true}` — `enabled` is the global switch *Start addons early when they support it* (on by default), `off` the addons it is switched off for, `next_boot` that a change applies at the next boot. `PUT`: `{enabled?, off?}` — a key left out keeps its value; `off` is cleaned to valid ids, each once, sorted. Rewrites every `addon-policy/<id>.start` at once (`PUT /addons/{id}/policy` below) and answers the `GET` view; nothing is restarted. `501` when the daemon runs without a configuration file (then every declaring addon starts early). The shell's switch is the Addons page's section *Addon start* (`/addons#addon-start`). |
| `PUT /addons/{id}/early-start` **`addons:write`** | `{enabled}` switches the early start for one addon — it enters or leaves `off`; the global switch is untouched. Answers the `GET /early-start` view plus `id`, `addon_enabled` (the addon's own switch), `start_early_declared` and `start_early` (it starts early at the next boot). `422` for an id that is not one or a body without `enabled`. The Addons page offers it in the ⋯ menu of an addon that declares the early start, and only there. |
| `PUT /addons/{id}/legacy-session` **`addons:write`** | **Administrator**: `{enabled}` switches the legacy session for one addon — it enters or leaves `off`; the global switch is untouched. Answers the `GET /legacy-session` view plus `id` and `addon_enabled` (the addon's effective state). `422` for an id that is not one. |
| `POST /addons/install` **`addons:write`** | Multipart `file` or a raw `.tar.gz` body → `/bin/install_addon`. **The manifest:** before the installer runs, occulited reads `openccu-lite.json` at the archive's root in Go (`archive/tar`, never a helper `tar -xOf`); after the installer it applies it to the addon it names when that addon's rc.d entry was created or changed by this install — the `runtime` block becomes the policy (`root` as declared, else the system's default mode; a mode the user chose on the Services page stands, only the block follows), the copy is kept root-owned as `addon-policy/<id>.manifest.json`, and `ui`, `requires` and `release` are read from that copy from then on; the file in the addon's own directory is never read again. A manifest whose `id` names an addon the install did not touch is not applied and the output says so; a broken one is ignored with a note. A package without a manifest takes the catalogue's manifest for the id (an adapter, or the one fetched for the page — read at the default branch, or at a release tag that is the installed version's) when there is one, source `catalog`; else the system's default mode and nothing more, as before — and the stored copy an earlier install left goes with the policy's `api_scopes` (`[manifest] <id>: … the stored declaration of the earlier install was removed`; the rest of the runtime block and the mode stay, occulited B-27). The output carries a `[manifest] …` line for what happened, and a `[lighttpd] <id>: the addon's lighttpd fragment was refused and is not in use: <reason> (line <n>: "<statement>")` line for every addon whose fragment fails the check after the installer ran — the sync covers all addons, so a verdict that stands from an earlier install is repeated (occulited task 23; the verdict stays on the addon's entry as `lighttpd_rejected`, `GET /addons`). **A job:** the request only stores the archive (422 `install-failed` when it is empty or larger than 300 MB; 409 `install-running` while an upload or a catalogue install runs, and `POST /catalog/{id}/install` answers the same 409 while an upload or another catalogue install runs) and answers **202** with the job `{id, state: "running", started, bytes}`; the install — the installer, the unit generation, the start — runs on a context of its own, so lighttpd's graceful reload during it (an addon's web fragment) cuts a request, never the install. `?wait=true` answers when the job ends instead (200 `done`, 422 `failed`; the install runs detached all the same). `GET /addons/install?job=<id>` **`system:read`** answers the job, without `job` the newest one (404 `unknown-job` for an id it does not know; without `job` and before the first install **204** without a body, occulited B-15; the last 8 are kept in memory, none survives a restart of occulited): `state` `running`, `done` (the installer ran and exited 0 or 10) or `failed` (it exited otherwise, `result` says how; or it did not run, `error` says why), `finished`, and `result` — `{exit, meaning, reboot_required, output, seconds, stopped_addons?}` with the CCU's exit-code semantics (0 ok, 10 reboot, 13 wrong platform, 101–106 installer errors). **A new addon** (its rc.d entry created by this install) is started in its unit after an installer that exited 0 or 10 (systemd) — a CCU starts it at the reboot exit 10 asks for; `reboot_required` stays the installer's word and the output says the addon was started without waiting for it. The job ends once that unit is active, or the output says the state it is in instead (`[systemd] <id> was started in its unit and is failed (exit-code) now: its log says why`; a unit still activating after 15 s is named so). `stopped_addons: [{id, starts_at_boot}]` (systemd): when the installer failed or asks for a reboot, no addon that was there before is started; these addons ran before the installer and their unit is not active afterwards, and the page asks whether to start each (the normal unit start). `starts_at_boot`: the next boot starts it anyway — its rc.d script is executable, its unit not switched off on the Services page, the system not in safe mode. A catalogue run's `result` in `/catalog/progress` carries the same field. |
| `POST /addons/install/local` *(open)* | openccu-lite B-274: the fork's `/bin/install_addon`, called outside occulited (on the command line, by an addon's own updater), hands its archive here as a raw body with `Authorization: Bearer <install token>`, so that the install is the same job as `POST /addons/install?wait=true` — the manifest, the policy, the rc.d wrapper adopted again, the unit stopped and started by systemd — instead of an update script's `rc.d/<id> start` leaving the addon running as its caller, root and unconfined. Registered without a scope; the handler checks the token (`/run/occulite/install-token`, root's alone, a new one at every start) and that the request comes from this system (not an address lighttpd forwards). **The answer is for a shell:** the output as `text/plain`, the installer's exit code in `X-Occulite-Addon-Exit` (1 when the installer did not run), status 200 (`done`), 422 (`failed`), 409 `install-running` (JSON) while another install runs, 401 without the token, from elsewhere, or when occulited has none. The fork's installer runs the install itself, as before, inside occulited's own install scope, when occulited does not answer, or when the token file is missing. |
| `POST /addons/{id}/remove-rc-entry` **`addons:write`** | occulited task 28: removes the rc.d entry of a script that came along from the CCU (a migrated rc.d script stays an addon and is started; a CCU3 user's own hack mostly fails here). `GET /addons` marks such an addon `from_ccu` (policy source `migrated`), with `failed_log` (the failed unit's last journal line) and `rc_target` (the regular file its link - the entry, or its `.script` twin behind the wrapper - leads to under `/usr/local/`, outside `/usr/local/addons/`, `/usr/local/etc/` and `/usr/local/var/`). The route stops `addon-<id>.service`, removes the entry and its `.script` twin through the helper (`RemoveAddonEntry`), with `{"target": true}` also `rc_target` (the helper's `rctargetremove`: one regular file, never a link or a directory, never followed), the addon's policy files as an uninstall does, then `daemon-reload` and `reset-failed` - no failed unit remains. → `{ok, removed, target}`; `422` for an addon with a directory of its own (uninstall it instead) or an unknown one, `501` without systemd. The Addons page asks first, with the target as a separate choice. |
| `POST /addons/{id}/enable`, `POST /addons/{id}/disable` **`addons:write`** | The rc.d script's executable bit — busybox `run-parts` and the systemd generator both skip a non-executable script — then start/stop. **On systemd, `disable`** (B-58) stops the unit, reloads the generator (the unit disappears) and runs `systemctl reset-failed` on it, so a disabled addon leaves no `not-found failed` unit behind (and no `degraded` system); a stop that fails is `control_error`. The first-boot steps below do the same for every addon they disabled at that start - the stop also cancels the start job `addons.target` queued before a slow scan finished - and at every start occulited resets a disabled addon's unit that systemd still lists as `not-found` and `failed`; `GET /services` leaves such a unit out. Addons that need the ReGa (`rega_dependent`, `rega_reason` in `/addons`: a built-in list plus a scan of the addon's code for ReGa idioms) are disabled on the first boot after an update from OpenCCU (`first_boot_import.disabled_addons`) and shown as "disabled, incompatible"; enabling one is the user's call. Addons whose binaries this system cannot execute (`binary_incompatible`, `binary_reason`: the ELF machine, word size and `PT_INTERP` loader of every file in the addon against the system's native ABI plus the 32-bit one when its loader exists) are disabled on the same first boot (`first_boot_import.disabled_binary_addons`) and shown as "disabled, needs update" — the repair is a release for this architecture, see [addons.md](https://github.com/hobbyquaker/openccu-lite/blob/main/docs/addons.md#architecture-and-addon-binaries). |
| ~~`GET /cleanup`, `POST /cleanup`~~ | **Removed (2026-09-25)** with the Backup page's *Leftovers from OpenCCU* panel: the leftovers (the same list) go by themselves once after the first start, when the first-boot name import settled; `<state>/ccu-leftovers-removed.json` records the run, the journal line `ccu leftovers removed` the paths and the bytes freed (docs/switching.md). **The known-useless CCU remnants** (occulited task 29) go once as well, after the name import settled, recorded in `<state>/ccu-remnants-removed.json` (`{at, removed: [{path, bytes, files}]}`), each with a journal line `ccu remnants: removed` and its path and size: `<config>/addons/<id>` whose addon is gone (no rc.d entry nor `.script` twin, no `/usr/local/addons/<id>`, and no rc.d script naming it; never `www`) - meine-homematic.de's `addons/mh` on a CCU3 - the ReGa's crash dump `homematic.regadom.err`, and `/usr/local/eQ-3-Backup` when it holds no byte. The helper's `ccu-remnant-remove` takes only that list and checks the conditions itself. The hardening of world-writable config files logs one line per directory with the number of entries. **The NEO Server's watchdog line** (`*/5 * * * * /usr/local/addons/mediola/bin/watchdog` in `/usr/local/crontabs/root`, which the eQ-3 CCU3 firmware writes on every CCU3, the addon installed or not) is removed at every start while the NEO Server is not installed (B-60: crond ran the missing program every five minutes); the crontab's other lines stay, the journal says `crontab: the NEO Server's watchdog line removed`. |
| `POST /addons/{id}/uninstall` **`addons:write`** | The rc.d script's `uninstall`, then the entry is removed from `hm_addons.cfg` — what `cp_software.cgi` does. Answers `{ok, output, system_removed}`: `output` is the script's own output, unchanged; **`system_removed`** (openccu-lite B-283) lists what the system removed after the script, in order and only what was there — the rc.d entry, the www link, the emptied standard directories, the monit fragment, the `hm_addons.cfg` entry (as `hm_addons.cfg: <id>`) and, on systemd, **every `addon-policy/<id>.*`** (the policy, its drop-in, `.needs`, `.start`, the stored manifest), so a reinstall starts clean from its new manifest and the firewall closes the ports the policy had opened. The journal (`journalctl -t addon-install`) gets the script's lines, then one line with the same list - for a confined addon also that the script ran as `addon-<id>`, so its refused `rm` lines read as handled (occulited B-26) - then how it ended. **The addon's uid is not given up** (openccu-lite B-288): every uid handed to an addon is recorded in the **uid registry** `/usr/local/etc/config/addon-uids.json` (`{"uids": {"<id>": <uid>}}`, beside the policy directory, not in it), which neither the uninstall nor the sweep below touches; confining an addon takes its registered uid, else the next one above every uid the registry, a stored policy and the addon users in `/etc/passwd` hold, and records it before the policy is written (no record, no confinement). So a reinstall under the same id runs as its old uid - the files it left on the userfs stay its own - and no other addon is ever given that uid; a registered uid that another addon's stored policy holds is not shared, the addon gets a new one. At its start, before the sweep, occulited registers what the system holds and the registry lacks - the stored policies' uids, then this boot's `addon-<id>` users, then the owners (uid from 30000 up) of `/usr/local/addons/<id>` - logged as `addon uids: registered`. A confined addon's script runs as its user and its `rm` of the rc.d entry and its directories is refused (root owns the parents) — those *permission denied* lines stay in `output`, and the Addons page says the system removed the rest. At its start occulited also sweeps the policy files of every id with no rc.d entry (an uninstall before this binary, an addon removed by other means), logged as `addon policies: files of addons that are no longer installed removed`. A failed script answers `422 uninstall-failed` with the same `output` and `system_removed`. On systemd the addon's unit is stopped first — a stop that fails (a stop script that exits non-zero because its daemon is already gone) is logged and the uninstall goes on — and after the generators' reload `systemctl reset-failed addon-<id>.service` clears a failure the stop left, so no `not-found` unit stays listed as failed (B-236). **The output into the journal** (the systemd product): every install (`POST /addons/install`, `POST /catalog/{id}/install`) and uninstall occulited runs also writes the script's output to the journal once the script has ended, so a failed install can be read afterwards on the Log page (Tag *Addon installs*, `journalctl -t addon-install`): one entry per non-blank line at priority 6 (a long line split at 16 KiB, at most the last 2000 lines of a run after a note of how many were left out), then a summary `<id>: install finished, exit N (meaning)` (or `uninstall finished`) with `ADDON_EXIT=N`, priority 6 on success (install 0 or 10, uninstall 0) and 3 on failure; `<id>: install failed: <error>` at 3 when the script could not run. Every entry carries `SYSLOG_IDENTIFIER=addon-install`, `ADDON_ACTION=install\|uninstall` and `ADDON_ID=<id>` where the addon is known — a catalogue install always, an upload when its installer created or changed exactly one rc.d entry. Written by occulited, so they also show under Unit `occulited`. |
| `GET /addons/{id}/policy` **`system:read`** | systemd only: `{systemd, default_mode, policy: {id, mode: "root"|"confined", uid, user, runtime, source}, mode, source, undeclared}`. `default_mode` is what a newly installed addon gets on this system — `confined` unless `addons.default_mode` says `root`. `policy` is null for an addon that has none (installed before the systemd product, or before the once-per-system adoption ran); the flat `mode`, `source` and `undeclared` are what the UI shows and are defined for such an addon too: `mode: "root"` (no drop-in is generated, so it *is* root), `source: ""`, `undeclared: true`. `source` is `default`, `manifest` (the package's own `openccu-lite.json`), `catalog` (the catalogue's manifest standing in for a package without one), `user`, `migrated` (already installed when the confined default arrived) or `fallback` (confining failed). `undeclared` means no `runtime` block was ever applied — nobody has said what the addon needs. A block that holds nothing but `needs` counts as none: the start order is not a statement of what the addon needs to run (docs/catalog-format.md), so it leaves the addon `undeclared`; every other block counts as before, `{}` included. `policy.runtime` carries **`needs`** as it was declared — absent (undeclared), `[]` (no interface) or a list of `rfd`, `hmipserver`, `hs485d` — the start order the system writes to `<id>.needs` (`PUT` below), and **`start`** (`"early"`). **`start_early_declared`** and **`start_early`** are the addon's early start as `GET /addons` has them. A block that holds nothing but `needs`, `start` and `session` leaves the addon `undeclared`. Busybox: `{systemd: false}`. |
| `PUT /addons/{id}/policy` **`addons:write`** | `{mode: "root"|"confined", unsafe, restart}` rewrites `/usr/local/etc/config/addon-policy/<id>.{json,conf}` — for "confined" the user `addon-<id>` (uid from 30000 up, kept across firmware updates and across an uninstall: see the uid registry below) is created and the addon's directories chowned — its three standard ones, and its **data directories**: the runtime block's `data_dirs` plus the convention `/usr/local/<id>` when it exists and belongs to no other addon, each given to it through the helper (a walk by descriptors that follows no link and changes only entries with another owner) and put on `ReadWritePaths`; the list that survived the guard rails is stored as `data_dirs` on the policy. The take-over runs again at every start of occulited (`RefreshDataDirs`, after the catalogue refresh and before the drop-ins are rendered), so a system confined before it existed heals itself — reloads the generator and restarts `addon-<id>.service` unless `restart: false`. The restart is a stop before the change and a start after it: the unit is stopped and its cgroup watched until it is empty (a KILL by pid to what is still there after five seconds), a daemon of the addon that runs outside the unit is stopped by pid, and only then are the files chowned and the unit started as the new user, so what a root daemon writes on its way out (Mosquitto its persistence) is the addon's too. A refused mode stops nothing; a policy write that fails after the stop starts the unit again; ten seconds after a start as `addon-<id>` the check looks at its files again and the warnings are evaluated. With `restart: false` nothing is stopped. The stored runtime block (from the manifest) stays — and at every start every policy takes its stored manifest's current block before the drop-ins are rendered (`RefreshManifestRuntimes`: a binary that learned a key renders it; the mode is never changed by a start; an installed addon without a stored manifest adopts the catalogue's adapter when there is one) — and so does `open_ports` — the declared ports the user opened on the Firewall page (`PUT /firewall/addons/{id}/ports`). **`mode: "root"` needs `"unsafe": true`** and answers 422 `unsafe_not_confirmed` without it: confined is the default since 2026-09-07 and root is the opt-out, so it is not a mode a caller reaches by leaving a field out. The user and its group are appended in place to `/etc/passwd` and `/etc/group` by the helper's own operation (no busybox `adduser`/`addgroup`); confining an addon on an image without `occu-etc-writable.service` answers 422 with the read-only error and a hint naming that unit. **`<id>.needs`** sits beside the two: one line, `none` for a declared `[]`, else the declared ids out of `rfd`, `hmipserver`, `hs485d` (each once, in that order), and no file for an undeclared addon. The fork's occu-addons generator reads it at the next boot and orders `addon-<id>.service` after exactly those units — no file: after rfd and hmipserver, as before; `none`: right after the network. It is written from what the addon declares now — the stored manifest's `needs` when it has one, else the stored block's — by every policy write (an install, this `PUT`, the adoption) and again at every start of occulited (`RefreshAddonNeeds`, after `RefreshManifestRuntimes`), for every `source` and in either mode: the order is a fact about the addon, not a grant. A declaration reaches the files with the install or update that brings the manifest; nothing refreshes them from outside the package. An id the system does not know makes the declaration unusable: logged, the file removed, the addon keeps the default. A manifest whose `needs` names an id the system does not know is applied without a start order (logged). **`<id>.start`** sits beside `<id>.needs`: the one line `early` when the addon declares `runtime.start: "early"` (the stored manifest's word when it says anything, else the stored block's) and the early start is switched on for it (`GET /early-start`: globally, and not in `off`); no file otherwise. The occu-addons generator reads it at the next boot: such a unit is ordered only after the network, lighttpd, occulited and `occu-addons.service`, and not on its interfaces. No addon unit has `Wants=` on an interface unit (openccu-lite B-308): `needs` is ordering only, and an addon's start never starts a radio daemon. It is written by every policy write, at every start (`RefreshAddonStart`, after `RefreshAddonNeeds`) and when a switch changes; in either mode and for every `source`. Any other value of `runtime.start` declares nothing and is logged. **`start_early: bool`** in the body switches the addon's early start (as `PUT /addons/{id}/early-start` does) and restarts nothing; a body with it and without `mode` changes only the switch and answers `{policy, restarted: false, start_early_declared, start_early, next_boot: true}`. |
| *(systemd)* | Both scripts run in a transient scope (`systemd-run --scope`) instead of occulited's cgroup. **After an install every addon it touched is settled in its unit**: a new id, an id whose rc.d entry the installer changed or that is no longer the addon-rc wrapper (an update script's `ln -sf` over it), and an id with a process of its own (as for `stray` above) in this install's scope.
